========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, thank you. It’s, ah... it’s a bit difficult to put into words, to be perfectly honest with you. I’ve spent the better part of the last few weeks just debating whether I should even make this appointment. You see, I’ve always been a very practical man. Going all the way back to my graduate studies, and then throughout my entire career, I was the sort of person who believed that if you just put your head down, made a schedule, and applied a bit of rigor, you could overcome just about any hurdle. 

But lately, especially since I’ve stepped away from the workforce, things have shifted. I came in today because I’ve started to feel—and it’s not some sudden, dramatic crisis, but rather in a very quiet, incredibly stubborn way—that the best version of my life is simply behind me. It's this heavy, persistent realization that no amount of planning or discipline on my part is going to get me back to feeling like myself. I used to be able to logic my way out of a rut, to just organize my days better, but now I just keep looking at the years ahead and thinking that the good chapters are already written and closed. And I just don't know what to do with that feeling, because my usual tools for fixing things just aren't working anymore.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Sad... well, it's not quite like the sadness you might expect. I mean, I’m not breaking down in tears or having these dramatic, weeping moments. I’ve never really been that sort of person anyway, even when things were genuinely tough. But empty... yes, maybe empty is closer to the mark, or perhaps just... muted. 

Most days, it’s like there is this... this low, gray tone running underneath absolutely everything I do. And I do mean everything. It doesn't seem to matter what the activity is or how much I try to apply myself to it. I could be out in my studio—I try to spend time out there, just tinkering and trying to stay busy, keeping my hands moving to maintain some semblance of a routine now that I'm retired—and that gray feeling is just sitting right there on the workbench with me. 

Or even in the evenings, which is what really bothers me the most, if I'm being honest. I'll be sitting right across the table from my wife at dinner. She’s a wonderful woman, and she’ll be talking about her day, or the neighbors, or whatever is going on with our friends, and I’m looking at her, and I know I should be engaged. I know I should feel that normal warmth of a shared meal. But instead, that dull, heavy grayness is just... it's just humming along in the background, completely ignoring the fact that I'm supposed to be enjoying a nice evening. It’s almost like someone turned down the color saturation on my entire life, and I just can't seem to find the dial to turn it back up, no matter how hard I concentrate on the meal or the conversation.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's... it's a complicated question, isn't it? Because "pleasure" is such a strong word. I wouldn't say I've stopped doing things, exactly. Like I mentioned, I still go out to the studio. I have a whole setup out there—woodworking tools I’ve collected over the better part of three decades, a really beautiful lathe that I used to spend hours on, just turning bowls and table legs. I used to lose track of time out there. I'd go out after breakfast and suddenly my wife would be calling me in for supper, and I'd realize I hadn't even stopped for lunch. 

I still go out there. I make it a point to go out there every morning at nine o'clock, right after I finish my coffee and the morning paper. I put on my apron, I select a piece of wood—maybe a nice piece of cherry or walnut—and I turn on the equipment. But... the feeling just isn't the same. I'm going through the motions, applying the chisels, sanding the grain down, but that sense of satisfaction, that quiet thrill of seeing something take shape under my hands... it's just not there. I finish a bowl, I set it on the shelf with the others, and I just feel... nothing in particular about it. 

And it's not just the woodworking. I used to really enjoy reading history biographies. I have a whole library of them in the den. I'd get so absorbed in the political maneuvering of the 19th century, or the strategic decisions during the World Wars. Now, I can sit in my favorite leather chair, open a book about Churchill or Lincoln, and I might read ten pages before I realize I haven't absorbed a single word. I'm just looking at the letters on the page. The interest, the spark that used to make me want to turn the page and see what happens next, even though I already know the history... it's just absent. So, I suppose I'm still doing the activities, but the enjoyment I used to extract from them... that seems to have evaporated somewhere along the line, despite my best efforts to keep up the routine.

I put on my apron, I select a piece of wood—maybe a nice piece of cherry or walnut—and I turn on the equipment. But... the feeling just isn't the same. I'm going through the motions, applying the chisels, sanding the grain down, but that sense of satisfaction, that quiet thrill of seeing something take shape under my hands... it's just not there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, that’s... that’s a very good question, and one I’ve actually spent quite a bit of time trying to pinpoint myself. Because, as I said, it wasn't a sudden drop-off. It wasn't like waking up one morning and finding the world had lost its color. It was much more insidious than that. 

But if I really trace it back, if I look at the timeline of when things started to feel like a chore rather than a life... it goes back to a hiking trip. My wife and I used to be quite active, you see. We loved the trails up in the state park, the ones with the really steep inclines that give you that wonderful view of the valley. We'd go every autumn when the leaves were turning. But a little over three years ago—it was October, so about three years and three months ago now, to be precise—I took a bad step on a loose rock coming down the ridge. 

It was a silly mistake, really. I had good boots on, I was using my poles, but I just lost my footing. I ended up with a rather nasty injury to my knee. Nothing life-threatening, of course, but it required surgery and a very, very long, tedious physical therapy process. And I remember sitting in that recliner in our living room with my leg elevated on these two specific pillows my wife bought just for that purpose, watching the snow fall outside that winter, and realizing I couldn't get out to my studio. I couldn't walk the dog. I couldn't do much of anything except read, and as I mentioned, even that started to lose its appeal. 

And that’s when I think this... this grayness really settled in. I kept telling myself, "Once the knee heals, once you're back on your feet, the fog will lift." And the knee did heal, eventually. I mean, it still aches when it rains, but I can walk perfectly fine now. But the fog... the fog never lifted. It just stayed right there, long after the physical therapy appointments ended. So yes, it’s been a little over three years now that I’ve been carrying this around with me. Three years of just... going through the motions.

I know I should feel that normal warmth of a shared meal. But instead, that dull, heavy grayness is just... it's just humming along in the background, completely ignoring the fact that I'm supposed to be enjoying a nice evening. It’s almost like someone turned down the color saturation on my entire life, and I just can't seem to find the dial to turn it back up, no matter how hard I concentrate on the meal or the conversation.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're no longer enjoying activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's a complicated question, isn't it? Because \"pleasure\" is such a strong word. I wouldn't say I've stopped doing things, exactly. Like I mentioned, I still go out to the studio. I have a whole setup out there—woodworking tools I’ve collected over the better part of three decades, a really beautiful lathe that I used to spend hours on, just turning bowls and table legs. I used to lose track of time out there. I'd go out after breakfast and suddenly my wife would be calling me in for supper, and I'd realize I hadn't even stopped for lunch. \n\nI still go out there. I make it a point to go out there every morning at nine o'clock, right after I finish my coffee and the morning paper. I put on my apron, I select a piece of wood—maybe a nice piece of cherry or walnut—and I turn on the equipment. But... the feeling just isn't the same. I'm going through the motions, applying the chisels, sanding the grain down, but that sense of satisfaction, that quiet thrill of seeing something take shape under my hands... it's just not there. I finish a bowl, I set it on the shelf with the others, and I just feel... nothing in particular about it. \n\nAnd it's not just the woodworking. I used to really enjoy reading history biographies. I have a whole library of them in the den. I'd get so absorbed in the political maneuvering of the 19th century, or the strategic decisions during the World Wars. Now, I can sit in my favorite leather chair, open a book about Churchill or Lincoln, and I might read ten pages before I realize I haven't absorbed a single word. I'm just looking at the letters on the page. The interest, the spark that used to make me want to turn the page and see what happens next, even though I already know the history... it's just absent. So, I suppose I'm still doing the activities, but the enjoyment I used to extract from them... that seems to have evaporated somewhere along the line, despite my best efforts to keep up the routine.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with this gray, muted tone to your life?\n\nPatient: Well, that’s... that’s a very good question, and one I’ve actually spent quite a bit of time trying to pinpoint myself. Because, as I said, it wasn't a sudden drop-off. It wasn't like waking up one morning and finding the world had lost its color. It was much more insidious than that. \n\nBut if I really trace it back, if I look at the timeline of when things started to feel like a chore rather than a life... it goes back to a hiking trip. My wife and I used to be quite active, you see. We loved the trails up in the state park, the ones with the really steep inclines that give you that wonderful view of the valley. We'd go every autumn when the leaves were turning. But a little over three years ago—it was October, so about three years and three months ago now, to be precise—I took a bad step on a loose rock coming down the ridge. \n\nIt was a silly mistake, really. I had good boots on, I was using my poles, but I just lost my footing. I ended up with a rather nasty injury to my knee. Nothing life-threatening, of course, but it required surgery and a very, very long, tedious physical therapy process. And I remember sitting in that recliner in our living room with my leg elevated on these two specific pillows my wife bought just for that purpose, watching the snow fall outside that winter, and realizing I couldn't get out to my studio. I couldn't walk the dog. I couldn't do much of anything except read, and as I mentioned, even that started to lose its appeal. \n\nAnd that’s when I think this... this grayness really settled in. I kept telling myself, \"Once the knee heals, once you're back on your feet, the fog will lift.\" And the knee did heal, eventually. I mean, it still aches when it rains, but I can walk perfectly fine now. But the fog... the fog never lifted. It just stayed right there, long after the physical therapy appointments ended. So yes, it’s been a little over three years now that I’ve been carrying this around with me. Three years of just... going through the motions.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   },

Guilty? No, I wouldn't say guilty. It's not like I'm sitting around beating myself up over the past or feeling like I've committed some great wrong. I mean, I certainly have regrets, as I'm sure any man my age does—you don't get to be sixty-five without wishing you'd handled a few things differently along the way. Like that time back in '98 when I decided not to take that partnership offer at the firm because I thought the commute would be too taxing, and then the firm ended up expanding massively... but that's just life, isn't it? That's not what you're asking about. 

As for my current situation, this... this muted feeling... I don't feel guilty about it. It's more of a frustration, really. A profound, quiet frustration. I know my wife gets worried, and I suppose I feel a twinge of regret that I can't be the lively companion she married, the one who used to plan all those hiking trips and surprise her with weekend getaways to bed and breakfasts up the coast. But guilt implies I feel like I'm doing this on purpose, or that it's a moral failing on my part. And I don't think it is. I'm trying, you know? I'm going out to the studio every morning at nine, I'm sitting down for dinner, I'm attempting to read my biographies. I'm doing everything I'm supposed to do to keep the engine running, so to speak. It's just that the engine isn't producing any power. So no, I don't sit in my leather chair reproaching myself. I just sit there wondering why the effort I'm putting in isn't yielding any sort of return.

I had good boots on, I was using my poles, but I just lost my footing. I ended up with a rather nasty injury to my knee. Nothing life-threatening, of course, but it required surgery and a very, very long, tedious physical therapy process. And I remember sitting in that recliner in our living room with my leg elevated on these two specific pillows my wife bought just for that purpose, watching the snow fall outside that winter, and realizing I couldn't get out to my studio. I couldn't walk the dog. I couldn't do much of anything except read, and as I mentioned, even that started to lose its appeal. \n\nAnd that’s when I think this... this grayness really settled in. I kept telling myself, \"Once the knee heals, once you're back on your feet, the fog will lift.\" And the knee did heal, eventually. I mean, it still aches when it rains, but I can walk perfectly fine now. But the fog... the fog never lifted. It just stayed right there, long after the physical therapy appointments ended. So yes, it’s been a little over three years now that I’ve been carrying this around with me. Three years of just... going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling guilty or reproaching yourself for things that have happened or for your current situation?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No, I wouldn't say guilty. It's not like I'm sitting around beating myself up over the past or feeling like I've committed some great wrong. I mean, I certainly have regrets, as I'm sure any man my age does—you don't get to be sixty-five without wishing you'd handled a few things differently along the way. Like that time back in '98 when I decided not to take that partnership offer at the firm because I thought the commute would be too taxing, and then the firm ended up expanding massively... but that's just life, isn't it? That's not what you're asking about. \n\nAs for my current situation, this... this muted feeling... I don't feel guilty about it. It's more of a frustration, really. A profound, quiet frustration. I know my wife gets worried, and I suppose I feel a twinge of regret that I can't be the lively companion she married, the one who used to plan all those hiking trips and surprise her with weekend getaways to bed and breakfasts up the coast. But guilt implies I feel like I'm doing this on purpose, or that it's a moral failing on my part. And I don't think it is. I'm trying, you know? I'm going out to the studio every morning at nine, I'm sitting down for dinner, I'm attempting to read my biographies. I'm doing everything I'm supposed to do to keep the engine running, so to speak. It's just that the engine isn't producing any power. So no, I don't sit in my leather chair reproaching myself. I just sit there wondering why the effort I'm putting in isn't yielding any sort of return.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I put on my apron, I select a piece of wood—maybe a nice piece of cherry or walnut—and I turn on the equipment. But... the feeling just isn't the same. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, that’s the rub, isn't it? I’ve been racking my brain trying to find something—anything, really—that sparks that old feeling. You’d think, being retired, having all this free time and a comfortable pension, I’d be jumping out of bed to seize the day. 

Take my daughter, for example. She comes over most Sunday afternoons with our two grandchildren. Now, any grandfather worth his salt is supposed to look forward to that all week, right? My wife spends two days prepping for it, baking cookies, getting a roast ready—the whole house smells like rosemary and garlic by noon on Sunday. And the kids, they come bursting through the front door, the dog gets all riled up and starts barking, it’s this whole grand production. It’s exactly the sort of family picture I worked all those decades at the firm to be able to sit back and enjoy in my twilight years. 

But... if I'm being completely honest with you, and I suppose that's why I'm sitting in this office... I don't actually look forward to it. I see it marked on the calendar in the kitchen, and instead of anticipation, I just feel... well, tired, I suppose. I think about the noise, and the sheer amount of energy it’s going to require to sit there and smile and ask the boys about their little league games and pretend I'm engaged. I love them, obviously. I'm not a monster, I care for my family deeply. But that actual feeling of joy? That little flutter in your chest when you know something good is about to happen? I just don't have it. 

And it's the same for the little things, too. I used to love just walking down the driveway to get the mail. I'd enjoy the crisp morning air, maybe chat with Bob, our postman—he's been on our route for fifteen years, good man, his son just graduated from state college. But now, even walking down the driveway is just a chore. It's just another thing I have to do. Not for Sunday dinners, not for my woodworking, not even for a quiet evening on the porch with a glass of iced tea. It’s all just... tasks on a list now. Boxes to check before the sun goes down. So, no, to answer your question. I really can't think of a single thing I genuinely look forward to anymore.

Going all the way back to my graduate studies, and then throughout my entire career, I was the sort of person who believed that if you just put your head down, made a schedule, and applied a bit of rigor, you could overcome just about any hurdle. \n\nBut lately, especially since I’ve stepped away from the workforce, things have shifted. I came in today because I’ve started to feel—and it’s not some sudden, dramatic crisis, but rather in a very quiet, incredibly stubborn way—that the best version of my life is simply behind me. It's this heavy, persistent realization that no amount of planning or discipline on my part is going to get me back to feeling like myself. I used to be able to logic my way out of a rut, to just organize my days better, but now I just keep looking at the years ahead and thinking that the good chapters are already written and closed. And I just don't know what to do with that feeling, because my usual tools for fixing things just aren't working anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Sad... well, it's not quite like the sadness you might expect. I mean, I’m not breaking down in tears or having these dramatic, weeping moments. I’ve never really been that sort of person anyway, even when things were genuinely tough. But empty... yes, maybe empty is closer to the mark, or perhaps just... muted. \n\nMost days, it’s like there is this... this low, gray tone running underneath absolutely everything I do. And I do mean everything. It doesn't seem to matter what the activity is or how much I try to apply myself to it. I'll be sitting right across the table from my wife at dinner. She’s a wonderful woman, and she’ll be talking about her day, or the neighbors, or whatever is going on with our friends, and I’m looking at her, and I know I should be engaged. I know I should feel that normal warmth of a shared meal. But instead, that dull, heavy grayness is just... it's just humming along in the background, completely ignoring the fact that I'm supposed to be enjoying a nice evening. It’s almost like someone turned down the color saturation on my entire life, and I just can't seem to find the dial to turn it back up, no matter how hard I concentrate on the meal or the conversation.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're no longer enjoying activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's a complicated question, isn't it? Because \"pleasure\" is such a strong word. I wouldn't say I've stopped doing things, exactly. Like I mentioned, I still go out to the studio. I have a whole setup out there—woodworking tools I’ve collected over the better part of three decades, a really beautiful lathe that I used to spend hours on, just turning bowls and table legs. I used to lose track of time out there. I'd go out after breakfast and suddenly my wife would be calling me in for supper, and I'd realize I hadn't even stopped for lunch. \n\nI still go out there. I make it a point to go out there every morning at nine o'clock, right after I finish my coffee and the morning paper. I put on my apron, I select a piece of wood—maybe a nice piece of cherry or walnut—and I turn on the equipment. But... the feeling just isn't the same. I'm going through the motions, applying the chisels, sanding the grain down, but that sense of satisfaction, that quiet thrill of seeing something take shape under my hands... it's just not there. I finish a bowl, I set it on the shelf with the others, and I just feel... nothing in particular about it. \n\nAnd it's not just the woodworking. I used to really enjoy reading history biographies. I have a whole library of them in the den. I'd get so absorbed in the political maneuvering of the 19th century, or the strategic decisions during the World Wars. Now, I can sit in my favorite leather chair, open a book about Churchill or Lincoln, and I might read ten pages before I realize I haven't absorbed a single word. I'm just looking at the letters on the page. The interest, the spark that used to make me want to turn the page and see what happens next, even though I already know the history... it's just absent. So, I suppose I'm still doing the activities, but the enjoyment I used to extract from them... that seems to have evaporated somewhere along the line, despite my best efforts to keep up the routine.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with this gray, muted tone to your life?\n\nPatient: Well, that’s... that’s a very good question, and one I’ve actually spent quite a bit of time trying to pinpoint myself. Because, as I said, it wasn't a sudden drop-off. It wasn't like waking up one morning and finding the world had lost its color. It was much more insidious than that. \n\nBut if I really trace it back, if I look at the timeline of when things started to feel like a chore rather than a life... it goes back to a hiking trip. My wife and I used to be quite active, you see. We loved the trails up in the state park, the ones with the really steep inclines that give you that wonderful view of the valley. We'd go every autumn when the leaves were turning. But a little over three years ago—it was October, so about three years and three months ago now, to be precise—I took a bad step on a loose rock coming down the ridge. \n\nIt was a silly mistake, really. I had good boots on, I was using my poles, but I just lost my footing. I ended up with a rather nasty injury to my knee. Nothing life-threatening, of course, but it required surgery and a very, very long, tedious physical therapy process. And I remember sitting in that recliner in our living room with my leg elevated on these two specific pillows my wife bought just for that purpose, watching the snow fall outside that winter, and realizing I couldn't get out to my studio. I couldn't walk the dog. I couldn't do much of anything except read, and as I mentioned, even that started to lose its appeal. \n\nAnd that’s when I think this... this grayness really settled in. I kept telling myself, \"Once the knee heals, once you're back on your feet, the fog will lift.\" And the knee did heal, eventually. It's just that the engine isn't producing any power. So no, I don't sit in my leather chair reproaching myself. I kept telling myself, \"Once the knee heals, once you're back on your feet, the fog will lift.\" And the knee did heal, eventually. I mean, it still aches when it rains, but I can walk perfectly fine now. But the fog... the fog never lifted. It just stayed right there, long after the physical therapy appointments ended. So yes, it’s been a little over three years now that I’ve been carrying this around with me. Three years of just... going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling guilty or reproaching yourself for things that have happened or for your current situation?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No, I wouldn't say guilty. It's not like I'm sitting around beating myself up over the past or feeling like I've committed some great wrong. I mean, I certainly have regrets, as I'm sure any man my age does—you don't get to be sixty-five without wishing you'd handled a few things differently along the way. Like that time back in '98 when I decided not to take that partnership offer at the firm because I thought the commute would be too taxing, and then the firm ended up expanding massively... but that's just life, isn't it? That's not what you're asking about. \n\nAs for my current situation, this... this muted feeling... I don't feel guilty about it. It's more of a frustration, really. A profound, quiet frustration. I know my wife gets worried, and I suppose I feel a twinge of regret that I can't be the lively companion she married, the one who used to plan all those hiking trips and surprise her with weekend getaways to bed and breakfasts up the coast. But guilt implies I feel like I'm doing this on purpose, or that it's a moral failing on my part. And I don't think it is. I'm trying, you know? I'm going out to the studio every morning at nine, I'm sitting down for dinner, I'm attempting to read my biographies. I'm doing everything I'm supposed to do to keep the engine running, so to speak. It's just that the engine isn't producing any power. So no, I don't sit in my leather chair reproaching myself. I just sit there wondering why the effort I'm putting in isn't yielding any sort of return.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Well, that’s the rub, isn't it? I’ve been racking my brain trying to find something—anything, really—that sparks that old feeling. You’d think, being retired, having all this free time and a comfortable pension, I’d be jumping out of bed to seize the day. \n\nTake my daughter, for example. She comes over most Sunday afternoons with our two grandchildren. Now, any grandfather worth his salt is supposed to look forward to that all week, right? My wife spends two days prepping for it, baking cookies, getting a roast ready—the whole house smells like rosemary and garlic by noon on Sunday. And the kids, they come bursting through the front door, the dog gets all riled up and starts barking, it’s this whole grand production. It’s exactly the sort of family picture I worked all those decades at the firm to be able to sit back and enjoy in my twilight years. \n\nBut... if I'm being completely honest with you, and I suppose that's why I'm sitting in this office... I don't actually look forward to it. I see it marked on the calendar in the kitchen, and instead of anticipation, I just feel... well, tired, I suppose. I think about the noise, and the sheer amount of energy it’s going to require to sit there and smile and ask the boys about their little league games and pretend I'm engaged. I love them, obviously. I'm not a monster, I care for my family deeply. But that actual feeling of joy? That little flutter in your chest when you know something good is about to happen? I just don't have it. \n\nAnd it's the same for the little things, too. I used to love just walking down the driveway to get the mail. I'd enjoy the crisp morning air, maybe chat with Bob, our postman—he's been on our route for fifteen years, good man, his son just graduated from state college. But now, even walking down the driveway is just a chore. It's just another thing I have to do. Not for Sunday dinners, not for my woodworking, not even for a quiet evening on the porch with a glass of iced tea. It’s all just... tasks on a list now. Boxes to check before the sun goes down. So, no, to answer your question. I really can't think of a single thing I genuinely look forward to anymore.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed a decrease in your ability to experience pleasure from activities you once enjoyed?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`.   },

Going all the way back to my graduate studies, and then throughout my entire career, I was the sort of person who believed that if you just put your head down, made a schedule, and applied a bit of rigor, you could overcome just about any hurdle. \n\nBut lately, especially since I’ve stepped away from the workforce, things have shifted. I came in today because I’ve started to feel—and it’s not some sudden, dramatic crisis, but rather in a very quiet, incredibly stubborn way—that the best version of my life is simply behind me. It's this heavy, persistent realization that no amount of planning or discipline on my part is going to get me back to feeling like myself. I used to be able to logic my way out of a rut, to just organize my days better, but now I just keep looking at the years ahead and thinking that the good chapters are already written and closed. And I just don't know what to do with that feeling, because my usual tools for fixing things just aren't working anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Sad... well, it's not quite like the sadness you might expect. I mean, I’m not breaking down in tears or having these dramatic, weeping moments. I’ve never really been that sort of person anyway, even when things were genuinely tough. But empty... yes, maybe empty is closer to the mark, or perhaps just... muted. \n\nMost days, it’s like there is this... this low, gray tone running underneath absolutely everything I do. And I do mean everything. It doesn't seem to matter what the activity is or how much I try to apply myself to it. I could be out in my studio—I try to spend time out there, just tinkering and trying to stay busy, keeping my hands moving to maintain some semblance of a routine now that I'm retired—and that gray feeling is just sitting right there on the workbench with me. \n\nOr even in the evenings, which is what really bothers me the most, if I'm being honest. I'll be sitting right across the table from my wife at dinner. She’s a wonderful woman, and she’ll be talking about her day, or the neighbors, or whatever is going on with our friends, and I’m looking at her, and I know I should be engaged. I know I should feel that normal warmth of a shared meal. But instead, that dull, heavy grayness is just... it's just humming along in the background, completely ignoring the fact that I'm supposed to be enjoying a nice evening. It’s almost like someone turned down the color saturation on my entire life, and I just can't seem to find the dial to turn it back up, no matter how hard I concentrate on the meal or the conversation.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're no longer enjoying activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's a complicated question, isn't it? Because \"pleasure\" is such a strong word. I wouldn't say I've stopped doing things, exactly. Like I mentioned, I still go out to the studio. I have a whole setup out there—woodworking tools I’ve collected over the better part of three decades, a really beautiful lathe that I used to spend hours on, just turning bowls and table legs. I used to lose track of time out there. I'd go out after breakfast and suddenly my wife would be calling me in for supper, and I'd realize I hadn't even stopped for lunch. \n\nI still go out there. I make it a point to go out there every morning at nine o'clock, right after I finish my coffee and the morning paper. I put on my apron, I select a piece of wood—maybe a nice piece of cherry or walnut—and I turn on the equipment. But... the feeling just isn't the same. I'm going through the motions, applying the chisels, sanding the grain down, but that sense of satisfaction, that quiet thrill of seeing something take shape under my hands... it's just not there. I finish a bowl, I set it on the shelf with the others, and I just feel... nothing in particular about it. \n\nAnd it's not just the woodworking. I used to really enjoy reading history biographies. I have a whole library of them in the den. I'd get so absorbed in the political maneuvering of the 19th century, or the strategic decisions during the World Wars. Now, I can sit in my favorite leather chair, open a book about Churchill or Lincoln, and I might read ten pages before I realize I haven't absorbed a single word. I'm just looking at the letters on the page. The interest, the spark that used to make me want to turn the page and see what happens next, even though I already know the history... it's just absent. So, I suppose I'm still doing the activities, but the enjoyment I used to extract from them... that seems to have evaporated somewhere along the line, despite my best efforts to keep up the routine.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with this gray, muted tone to your life?\n\nPatient: Well, that’s... that’s a very good question, and one I’ve actually spent quite a bit of time trying to pinpoint myself. Because, as I said, it wasn't a sudden drop-off. It wasn't like waking up one morning and finding the world had lost its color. It was much more insidious than that. \n\nBut if I really trace it back, if I look at the timeline of when things started to feel like a chore rather than a life... it goes back to a hiking trip. My wife and I used to be quite active, you see. We loved the trails up in the state park, the ones with the really steep inclines that give you that wonderful view of the valley. We'd go every autumn when the leaves were turning. But a little over three years ago—it was October, so about three years and three months ago now, to be precise—I took a bad step on a loose rock coming down the ridge. \n\nIt was a silly mistake, really. I had good boots on, I was using my poles, but I just lost my footing. I ended up with a rather nasty injury to my knee. Nothing life-threatening, of course, but it required surgery and a very, very long, tedious physical therapy process. And I remember sitting in that recliner in our living room with my leg elevated on these two specific pillows my wife bought just for that purpose, watching the snow fall outside that winter, and realizing I couldn't get out to my studio. I couldn't walk the dog. I couldn't do much of anything except read, and as I mentioned, even that started to lose its appeal. \n\nAnd that’s when I think this... this grayness really settled in. I kept telling myself, \"Once the knee heals, once you're back on your feet, the fog will lift.\" And the knee did heal, eventually. I mean, it still aches when it rains, but I can walk perfectly fine now. But the fog... the fog never lifted. It just stayed right there, long after the physical therapy appointments ended. So yes, it’s been a little over three years now that I’ve been carrying this around with me. Three years of just... going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling guilty or reproaching yourself for things that have happened or for your current situation?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No, I wouldn't say guilty. It's not like I'm sitting around beating myself up over the past or feeling like I've committed some great wrong. I mean, I certainly have regrets, as I'm sure any man my age does—you don't get to be sixty-five without wishing you'd handled a few things differently along the way. Like that time back in '98 when I decided not to take that partnership offer at the firm because I thought the commute would be too taxing, and then the firm ended up expanding massively... but that's just life, isn't it? That's not what you're asking about. \n\nAs for my current situation, this... this muted feeling... I don't feel guilty about it. It's more of a frustration, really. A profound, quiet frustration. I know my wife gets worried, and I suppose I feel a twinge of regret that I can't be the lively companion she married, the one who used to plan all those hiking trips and surprise her with weekend getaways to bed and breakfasts up the coast. But guilt implies I feel like I'm doing this on purpose, or that it's a moral failing on my part. And I don't think it is. I'm trying, you know? I'm going out to the studio every morning at nine, I'm sitting down for dinner, I'm attempting to read my biographies. I'm doing everything I'm supposed to do to keep the engine running, so to speak. It's just that the engine isn't producing any power. So no, I don't sit in my leather chair reproaching myself. I just sit there wondering why the effort I'm putting in isn't yielding any sort of return.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Well, that’s the rub, isn't it? I’ve been racking my brain trying to find something—anything, really—that sparks that old feeling. You’d think, being retired, having all this free time and a comfortable pension, I’d be jumping out of bed to seize the day. \n\nTake my daughter, for example. She comes over most Sunday afternoons with our two grandchildren. Now, any grandfather worth his salt is supposed to look forward to that all week, right? My wife spends two days prepping for it, baking cookies, getting a roast ready—the whole house smells like rosemary and garlic by noon on Sunday. And the kids, they come bursting through the front door, the dog gets all riled up and starts barking, it’s this whole grand production. It’s exactly the sort of family picture I worked all those decades at the firm to be able to sit back and enjoy in my twilight years. \n\nBut... if I'm being completely honest with you, and I suppose that's why I'm sitting in this office... I don't actually look forward to it. I see it marked on the calendar in the kitchen, and instead of anticipation, I just feel... well, tired, I suppose. I think about the noise, and the sheer amount of energy it’s going to require to sit there and smile and ask the boys about their little league games and pretend I'm engaged. I love them, obviously. I'm not a monster, I care for my family deeply. But that actual feeling of joy? That little flutter in your chest when you know something good is about to happen? I just don't have it. \n\nAnd it's the same for the little things, too. I used to love just walking down the driveway to get the mail. I'd enjoy the crisp morning air, maybe chat with Bob, our postman—he's been on our route for fifteen years, good man, his son just graduated from state college. But now, even walking down the driveway is just a chore. It's just another thing I have to do. Not for Sunday dinners, not for my woodworking, not even for a quiet evening on the porch with a glass of iced tea. It’s all just... tasks on a list now. Boxes to check before the sun goes down. So, no, to answer your question. I really can't think of a single thing I genuinely look forward to anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your ability to experience pleasure from activities you once enjoyed?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm... I'm not entirely sure what you mean by that, doctor, or how that's fundamentally different from what we were just discussing. I mean, I feel like I just answered that, didn't I? I just told you all about the Sunday dinners with my daughter and the grandchildren, and how I don't get any joy out of them anymore. So if you're asking if there's been a decrease... well, yes, obviously. That's the whole crux of the matter. That's the gray, muted tone I've been trying to explain to you since I first sat down in this chair. \n\nI really don't have any new or different examples to give you, if that's what you're looking for. I've already walked you through how I go out to my woodworking studio and just sit there without actually wanting to make anything. I've told you about the family visits, the morning walks to the mailbox to see Bob... I haven't noticed any *other* specific areas of decrease, if that's what you're fishing for, because it feels like the decrease has already painted over everything I do. It's just a general, overarching lack of enjoyment in the very things I spent my entire career at the firm looking forward to doing in my retirement. \n\nSo, I'm not sure I can give you a better or more specific answer than the one I just gave you a moment ago. The pleasure is just... it's just not there. Across the board. I hope that makes sense, because I'm really not sure how else to explain it to you.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Doctor, I... I have to admit, I'm starting to feel like a broken record here, and I'm wondering if maybe I'm just not speaking clearly enough. We are essentially circling the exact same drain. When you say "no matter how small," I really did mean it when I said I've already thought about the little things. I mentioned the walk to the mailbox. I mentioned sitting with a glass of iced tea. 

But, alright, if we really need to drill down into the absolute minutiae of my day to be absolutely certain... let me walk you through it. Let's take an average Tuesday morning. I wake up. I go to the kitchen. I take out the coffee grinder. My wife buys these very nice, expensive Ethiopian beans from a little specialty roaster downtown—the one over by the old courthouse, you might know it. Used to be, just opening that bag and smelling the roast was a... well, a tiny little highlight. A thirty-second moment of genuine pleasure before the day began. Now? It's just noise. The grinder is just a loud noise, and the coffee is just a hot liquid I swallow out of sheer habit. 

Then I sit down with the morning paper. I used to get a real kick out of reading the editorial section, you know, really dissecting the op-eds. I spent forty years doing corporate litigation, so picking apart a poorly structured argument used to be my bread and butter. I'd read some politician's nonsense and feel a little spark of intellectual superiority, I suppose you could say. But now? I just look at the words. I read them, I comprehend them, and I feel absolutely nothing. I don't care enough to argue with the newspaper. I just fold it up and put it in the recycling bin. 

So, to answer your question for what feels like the third time... no. Not the big things like the grandchildren, not the medium things like my woodworking studio, and not the small things like the smell of expensive coffee or a well-written article. Nothing. I really don't know how else to explain it to you, doctor. Every single activity, regardless of its size or significance, is just completely flat.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F33.0, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, thank you. It’s, ah... it’s a bit difficult to put into words, to be perfectly honest with you. I’ve spent the better part of the last few weeks just debating whether I should even make this appointment. You see, I’ve always been a very practical man. Going all the way back to my graduate studies, and then throughout my entire career, I was the sort of person who believed that if you just put your head down, made a schedule, and applied a bit of rigor, you could overcome just about any hurdle. \n\nBut lately, especially since I’ve stepped away from the workforce, things have shifted. I came in today because I’ve started to feel—and it’s not some sudden, dramatic crisis, but rather in a very quiet, incredibly stubborn way—that the best version of my life is simply behind me. It's this heavy, persistent realization that no amount of planning or discipline on my part is going to get me back to feeling like myself. I used to be able to logic my way out of a rut, to just organize my days better, but now I just keep looking at the years ahead and thinking that the good chapters are already written and closed. And I just don't know what to do with that feeling, because my usual tools for fixing things just aren't working anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Sad... well, it's not quite like the sadness you might expect. I mean, I’m not breaking down in tears or having these dramatic, weeping moments. I’ve never really been that sort of person anyway, even when things were genuinely tough. But empty... yes, maybe empty is closer to the mark, or perhaps just... muted. \n\nMost days, it’s like there is this... this low, gray tone running underneath absolutely everything I do. And I do mean everything. It doesn't seem to matter what the activity is or how much I try to apply myself to it. I could be out in my studio—I try to spend time out there, just tinkering and trying to stay busy, keeping my hands moving to maintain some semblance of a routine now that I'm retired—and that gray feeling is just sitting right there on the workbench with me. \n\nOr even in the evenings, which is what really bothers me the most, if I'm being honest. I'll be sitting right across the table from my wife at dinner. She’s a wonderful woman, and she’ll be talking about her day, or the neighbors, or whatever is going on with our friends, and I’m looking at her, and I know I should be engaged. I know I should feel that normal warmth of a shared meal. But instead, that dull, heavy grayness is just... it's just humming along in the background, completely ignoring the fact that I'm supposed to be enjoying a nice evening. It’s almost like someone turned down the color saturation on my entire life, and I just can't seem to find the dial to turn it back up, no matter how hard I concentrate on the meal or the conversation.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're no longer enjoying activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's a complicated question, isn't it? Because \"pleasure\" is such a strong word. I wouldn't say I've stopped doing things, exactly. Like I mentioned, I still go out to the studio. I have a whole setup out there—woodworking tools I’ve collected over the better part of three decades, a really beautiful lathe that I used to spend hours on, just turning bowls and table legs. I used to lose track of time out there. I'd go out after breakfast and suddenly my wife would be calling me in for supper, and I'd realize I hadn't even stopped for lunch. \n\nI still go out there. I make it a point to go out there every morning at nine o'clock, right after I finish my coffee and the morning paper. I put on my apron, I select a piece of wood—maybe a nice piece of cherry or walnut—and I turn on the equipment. But... the feeling just isn't the same. I'm going through the motions, applying the chisels, sanding the grain down, but that sense of satisfaction, that quiet thrill of seeing something take shape under my hands... it's just not there. I finish a bowl, I set it on the shelf with the others, and I just feel... nothing in particular about it. \n\nAnd it's not just the woodworking. I used to really enjoy reading history biographies. I have a whole library of them in the den. I'd get so absorbed in the political maneuvering of the 19th century, or the strategic decisions during the World Wars. Now, I can sit in my favorite leather chair, open a book about Churchill or Lincoln, and I might read ten pages before I realize I haven't absorbed a single word. I'm just looking at the letters on the page. The interest, the spark that used to make me want to turn the page and see what happens next, even though I already know the history... it's just absent. So, I suppose I'm still doing the activities, but the enjoyment I used to extract from them... that seems to have evaporated somewhere along the line, despite my best efforts to keep up the routine.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with this gray, muted tone to your life?\n\nPatient: Well, that’s... that’s a very good question, and one I’ve actually spent quite a bit of time trying to pinpoint myself. Because, as I said, it wasn't a sudden drop-off. It wasn't like waking up one morning and finding the world had lost its color. It was much more insidious than that. \n\nBut if I really trace it back, if I look at the timeline of when things started to feel like a chore rather than a life... it goes back to a hiking trip. My wife and I used to be quite active, you see. We loved the trails up in the state park, the ones with the really steep inclines that give you that wonderful view of the valley. We'd go every autumn when the leaves were turning. But a little over three years ago—it was October, so about three years and three months ago now, to be precise—I took a bad step on a loose rock coming down the ridge. \n\nIt was a silly mistake, really. I had good boots on, I was using my poles, but I just lost my footing. I ended up with a rather nasty injury to my knee. Nothing life-threatening, of course, but it required surgery and a very, very long, tedious physical therapy process. And I remember sitting in that recliner in our living room with my leg elevated on these two specific pillows my wife bought just for that purpose, watching the snow fall outside that winter, and realizing I couldn't get out to my studio. I couldn't walk the dog. I couldn't do much of anything except read, and as I mentioned, even that started to lose its appeal. \n\nAnd that’s when I think this... this grayness really settled in. I kept telling myself, \"Once the knee heals, once you're back on your feet, the fog will lift.\" And the knee did heal, eventually. I mean, it still aches when it rains, but I can walk perfectly fine now. But the fog... the fog never lifted. It just stayed right there, long after the physical therapy appointments ended. So yes, it’s been a little over three years now that I’ve been carrying this around with me. Three years of just... going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling guilty or reproaching yourself for things that have happened or for your current situation?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No, I wouldn't say guilty. It's not like I'm sitting around beating myself up over the past or feeling like I've committed some great wrong. I mean, I certainly have regrets, as I'm sure any man my age does—you don't get to be sixty-five without wishing you'd handled a few things differently along the way. Like that time back in '98 when I decided not to take that partnership offer at the firm because I thought the commute would be too taxing, and then the firm ended up expanding massively... but that's just life, isn't it? That's not what you're asking about. \n\nAs for my current situation, this... this muted feeling... I don't feel guilty about it. It's more of a frustration, really. A profound, quiet frustration. I know my wife gets worried, and I suppose I feel a twinge of regret that I can't be the lively companion she married, the one who used to plan all those hiking trips and surprise her with weekend getaways to bed and breakfasts up the coast. But guilt implies I feel like I'm doing this on purpose, or that it's a moral failing on my part. And I don't think it is. I'm trying, you know? I'm going out to the studio every morning at nine, I'm sitting down for dinner, I'm attempting to read my biographies. I'm doing everything I'm supposed to do to keep the engine running, so to speak. It's just that the engine isn't producing any power. So no, I don't sit in my leather chair reproaching myself. I just sit there wondering why the effort I'm putting in isn't yielding any sort of return.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Well, that’s the rub, isn't it? I’ve been racking my brain trying to find something—anything, really—that sparks that old feeling. You’d think, being retired, having all this free time and a comfortable pension, I’d be jumping out of bed to seize the day. \n\nTake my daughter, for example. She comes over most Sunday afternoons with our two grandchildren. Now, any grandfather worth his salt is supposed to look forward to that all week, right? My wife spends two days prepping for it, baking cookies, getting a roast ready—the whole house smells like rosemary and garlic by noon on Sunday. And the kids, they come bursting through the front door, the dog gets all riled up and starts barking, it’s this whole grand production. It’s exactly the sort of family picture I worked all those decades at the firm to be able to sit back and enjoy in my twilight years. \n\nBut... if I'm being completely honest with you, and I suppose that's why I'm sitting in this office... I don't actually look forward to it. I see it marked on the calendar in the kitchen, and instead of anticipation, I just feel... well, tired, I suppose. I think about the noise, and the sheer amount of energy it’s going to require to sit there and smile and ask the boys about their little league games and pretend I'm engaged. I love them, obviously. I'm not a monster, I care for my family deeply. But that actual feeling of joy? That little flutter in your chest when you know something good is about to happen? I just don't have it. \n\nAnd it's the same for the little things, too. I used to love just walking down the driveway to get the mail. I'd enjoy the crisp morning air, maybe chat with Bob, our postman—he's been on our route for fifteen years, good man, his son just graduated from state college. But now, even walking down the driveway is just a chore. It's just another thing I have to do. Not for Sunday dinners, not for my woodworking, not even for a quiet evening on the porch with a glass of iced tea. It’s all just... tasks on a list now. Boxes to check before the sun goes down. So, no, to answer your question. I really can't think of a single thing I genuinely look forward to anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your ability to experience pleasure from activities you once enjoyed?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm... I'm not entirely sure what you mean by that, doctor, or how that's fundamentally different from what we were just discussing. I mean, I feel like I just answered that, didn't I? I just told you all about the Sunday dinners with my daughter and the grandchildren, and how I don't get any joy out of them anymore. So if you're asking if there's been a decrease... well, yes, obviously. That's the whole crux of the matter. That's the gray, muted tone I've been trying to explain to you since I first sat down in this chair. \n\nI really don't have any new or different examples to give you, if that's what you're looking for. I've already walked you through how I go out to my woodworking studio and just sit there without actually wanting to make anything. I've told you about the family visits, the morning walks to the mailbox to see Bob... I haven't noticed any *other* specific areas of decrease, if that's what you're fishing for, because it feels like the decrease has already painted over everything I do. It's just a general, overarching lack of enjoyment in the very things I spent my entire career at the firm looking forward to doing in my retirement. \n\nSo, I'm not sure I can give you a better or more specific answer than the one I just gave you a moment ago. The pleasure is just... it's just not there. Across the board. I hope that makes sense, because I'm really not sure how else to explain it to you.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities, no matter how small, that you still find give you some sense of pleasure?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I... I have to admit, I'm starting to feel like a broken record here, and I'm wondering if maybe I'm just not speaking clearly enough. We are essentially circling the exact same drain. When you say \"no matter how small,\" I really did mean it when I said I've already thought about the little things. I mentioned the walk to the mailbox. I mentioned sitting with a glass of iced tea. \n\nBut, alright, if we really need to drill down into the absolute minutiae of my day to be absolutely certain... let me walk you through it. Let's take an average Tuesday morning. I wake up. I go to the kitchen. I take out the coffee grinder. My wife buys these very nice, expensive Ethiopian beans from a little specialty roaster downtown—the one over by the old courthouse, you might know it. Used to be, just opening that bag and smelling the roast was a... well, a tiny little highlight. A thirty-second moment of genuine pleasure before the day began. Now? It's just noise. The grinder is just a loud noise, and the coffee is just a hot liquid I swallow out of sheer habit. \n\nThen I sit down with the morning paper. I used to get a real kick out of reading the editorial section, you know, really dissecting the op-eds. I spent forty years doing corporate litigation, so picking apart a poorly structured argument used to be my bread and butter. I'd read some politician's nonsense and feel a little spark of intellectual superiority, I suppose you could say. But now? I just look at the words. I read them, I comprehend them, and I feel absolutely nothing. I don't care enough to argue with the newspaper. I just fold it up and put it in the recycling bin. \n\nSo, to answer your question for what feels like the third time... no. Not the big things like the grandchildren, not the medium things like my woodworking studio, and not the small things like the smell of expensive coffee or a well-written article. Nothing. I really don't know how else to explain it to you, doctor. Every single activity, regardless of its size or significance, is just completely flat.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Well, yes. Yes, I do. But it’s... it’s a peculiar kind of tired, if I’m being precise. It’s not that I’m bedridden or incapable of functioning, so please don't get the wrong impression. I still get up at the same time every morning. I still do my share of the household errands—I do the grocery shopping on Tuesdays and Thursdays, for instance. My wife always insists I’m the one who goes because she claims I have a better eye for picking out the produce, which is rather ironic considering I spent forty years looking at legal briefs, not heirloom tomatoes. 

And, as I mentioned before, I still go out to the garage and tinker with my woodworking projects. I go through the motions. But the energy it takes to do these things... it’s entirely disproportionate to the task itself. 

Let me give you an example. I do a bit of volunteer mentoring for some of the local law students—just trying to pass on some practical knowledge about corporate litigation that they don't teach you in a textbook. It used to be effortless. Now, just the process of setting up for a mentoring session... arranging the chairs, making sure I have my notes in order, greeting the students... it leaves me completely worn out. Or if my wife wants to go to one of those community events they hold downtown in the square—the art fairs or the farmer's market—just walking through the event, navigating the stalls and the people... by the time we get back to the car, I feel like I've just argued a grueling three-week trial. 

And the frustrating part is that resting doesn't seem to cure it. I’ll come home from one of these outings, sit down in my recliner in the den, and try to sleep it off. But even if I do manage to drift off, a nap doesn’t really fix it. I wake up feeling just as heavy and drained as I did before I closed my eyes. It’s this persistent, bone-deep weariness that just follows me around, no matter what I’m doing or how much I rest.
